PREFACE

This Report has been prepared for submission to the Governor of

Rajasthan under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.

Chapter 1 of this Report contain audit observations on matters arising from
examination of the Finance Accounts of the State Government for the year

ended 31 March 2012.

Chapter 2 of this Report contain audit observations on matters arising from
examination of the Appropriation Accounts of the State Government for
the year ended 31 March 2012.

Chapter 3 on Financial Reporting provides an overview and status of the
State Government's compliance with various financial rules, procedures
and directives in the departments relating to Social, Economic, Revenue

and General Sectors during the current year.

Audit Report (State Finances)
Lond for the year ended 31 March 2012


Executive Summary

Background

Based on the audited accounts of the Government of Rajasthan (GoR) for the
year ending March 2012, this report provides an analytical review of the
Annual Accounts of the State Government. The financial performance of the
State has been assessed based on the Fiscal Responsibility and Budgetary
Management (FRBM) Act, Budget Documents, Economic Review 2011-12,
Thirteenth Finance Commission (XIII-FC) Report and other financial data
obtained from various Government departments and organisations. The report
is structured in three Chapters.

Chapter 1 is based on the audit of Finance Accounts and makes an
assessment of GoR's fiscal position as on 31 March 2012. It provides an
insight into trends in committed expenditure, borrowing pattern, besides a
brief account of Central funds transferred directly to the State Implementing
Agencies through off-budget route.

Chapter 2 is based on Appropriation Accounts and gives grant-by-grant
description of appropriations and the manner in which the allocated resources
were managed by the service delivery departments.

Chapter 3 is an inventory of GoR's compliance with various reporting
requirements and financial rules.

The report also has an Appendix I of additional data collected from several
sources in support of the findings. Appendix 4.1 at the end gives a glossary of
selected terms related to State economy, as used in this report.

Audit findings and recommendations

The State Government has shown a return to fiscal consolidation by
showing a healthy growth in both tax and non-tax revenue. The fiscal
deficit was a healthy one per cent of Gross State Domestic Product (GSDP)
as against the target of three per cent as prescribed by revised FRBM Act
for 2011-12.

Budget Estimates: The actual revenue receipts and revenue expenditure
increased by nine per cent and three per cent respectively as compared to the
budget estimates for 2011-12. Resultantly, the revenue surplus increased by
% 3,004 crore as compared to the budget estimates. The estimated primary
deficit also turned into primary surplus. The actual fiscal deficit declined from
the budget estimate by हैं 4,437 crore; deficit was one per cent of GSDP as
against 2.4 per cent of budget estimates.

Revenue Receipts: During 2011-12, the State’s revenue receipts increased by
24.1 per cent over the previous year. The State’s revenue receipts as a
percentage of GSDP marginally declined from 15.8 per cent in 2007-08 to
15.5 per cent in 2011-12. Government should mobilise additional resources

Audit Report (State Finances)
Lod for the year ended 31 March 2012


through tax and non-tax revenue by expanding the tax base and rationalising
the user charges.

Revenue expenditure: During 2011-12, the revenue expenditure constituted
87 per cent of the total expenditure while the Non-Plan Revenue Expenditure
constituted 77 per cent of Revenue Expenditure. Nearly 58 per cent of the
revenue receipts of the State was spent on committed expenditure. There is a
necessity to contain the committed expenditure.

Inadequate Priority to Capital expenditure: The capital expenditure
increased by 36 per cent over the previous year and it was 17 per cent lower
than that projected in Medium Term Fiscal Policy Statement (MTFPS) for
2011-12. The progressive capital expenditure at the end of March 2012
includes हैं 7,993 crore (11.7 per cent) blocked on 160 incomplete projects.
Government should give adequate priority to capital expenditure and should
formulate guidelines for early completion of incomplete projects to avoid time
and cost over runs with a view to take corrective action.

Review of Government investments: The average return on State
Government’s investment in Statutory Corporations, Rural Banks, Joint Stock
Companies and Co-operatives varied between 0.2 and 0.4 per cent in the past
three years while the Government paid an average interest of 7.7 per cent on
this investment. It would be prudent to review the working of State Public
Sector Undertakings which are incurring huge losses and work out either a
revival strategy (for those which can be made viable) or close them down (if.
they are not likely to be viable given the current market conditions).

Prudent cash management: The State had a huge surplus cash balance of
= 9,785 crore. Since, maintaining huge idle cash balance is not prudent cash
management, appropriate steps should be taken for spending on capital
projects for creation of assets.

Oversight of funds transferred directly from the "6ण to the State
Implementing Agencies: Gol directly transferred substantial amount of funds
to the State Implementing Agencies, which is fraught with the risk of their
improper utilisation by these agencies. There is no single agency monitoring
its use and there is no readily available data on how much is actually spent in
any particular year on major flagship schemes. A system has to be put in place
to ensure proper accounting of these funds and the updated information should
be validated by the State Government as well as the Principal Accountant
General.

A review of release and utilisation of grants recommended by XIII-FC by
some Departments revealed non-utilisation of grant of हैं 265 crore against
sanctioned amount of € 404 crore by eight departments due to non-finalisation
of action plan/work plans.

Financial Management and Budgetary Control: During 2011-12, there was
an overall saving of = 6,537.27 crore over the total grants and appropriations
indicating inaccurate budgeting. There was excess expenditure of € 0.12 crore
which requires regularisation under Article 205 of the Constitution of India. In
all 55 Grants/appropriations, 93 per cent of total savings was surrendered on

Audit Report (State Finances) vi
for the year ended 31 March 2012 Lod


the last working day of the financial year leaving no scope for utilising these
funds for other development purposes. In 79 cases, lump sum provision of
₹ 1,742.31 crore was made violating provisions of SBM. Of this, 90 per cent
remained unutilised. Rush of expenditure at the end of the year is another
chronic feature, which indicates weak budgetary control.

Review of some grants revealed that (i) the Food and Civil Supplies
Department transferred funds of € seven crore in PD Account on 31 March
2012 to meet out the requirement of 2012-13, (ii) there were persistent savings
in Forest, Medical and Health, Food and Civil Supplies, Agriculture and Water
Resources Departments, (iii) though the Tourism Department had been
making provision every year in the estimates, there were no receipts during
2009-12. The provision of % five crore was made by Tourism Department in
budget estimates without ensuring the availability of land; an amount of € 1.93
crore meant for utilisation on two project was lying unutilised in PD Accounts
of JDA, ADMA and State Government since November 2010. % 3.92 crore
released by Gol for conservation and restoration of Shiv Temple (District
Baran) was lying blocked with the State Government since December 2010
and (iv) in Labour and Employment Department, the State Government has
not transferred (March 2012) labour cess of हैं 229 crore to the Rajasthan
Building and other Construction Workers Welfare Board, Jaipur collected
during 2010-12. Inspection of treasuries indicated deficiencies in their
functioning. In 37 cases an expenditure of 88.80 crore was incurred on New
Services without the approval by the Legislature.

Departments should submit more realistic budget estimates keeping in view
the trends in expenditure and actual requirement of funds. Budgetary controls
should be strictly observed. Anticipated savings should be surrendered well in
time so that the funds are utilised for various development programmes.
Expenditure on New Service should be met from Contingency Fund and bear
approval of the Legislature. Functioning of Treasuries should be monitored to
avoid their deficient functioning.

Financial Reporting: State Government’s compliance with various rules,
procedures and directives was unsatisfactory as evident from delays in
furnishing utilisation certificates against the loans and grants, from various
grantee institutions. Annual Accounts in respect of 131 Autonomous
Bodies/Authorities for the year 2010-11 have not been received upto 31 March
2012. There were instances of losses and misappropriations of Government
money amounting to हैं 44.07 crore as of 31 March 2012. Departmental
enquiries in such cases should be expedited to bring the defaulters to book.
Internal controls in all the organisations should be strengthened to prevent
recurrence of such cases in future.

The Controlling Officers did not submit the Detailed Contingent Bills in
respect of हैं 62.70 crore against the advances drawn on 441 Abstract
Contingent Bills upto 31 March 2012. Besides, unspent balance of % 2,016.65
crore was lying in 1,877 Personal Deposit (PD) Accounts.

A rigorous monitoring mechanism should be put in place by the DDOs to
adjust the abstract contingent bills in time and maintain the PD Accounts
properly, as required under the extant rules.

for the year ended 31 March 2012



Significant amounts of expenditure (¥ 6,889.20 crore) and receipts (€ 2,030.32
crore) under Central and State schemes, booked under the Minor head
*800-Other expenditure/other receipts’ were not depicted in the appropriate
head of account in Finance Accounts of 2011-12, affecting the transparency in
financial reporting. Amounts received and expended under various schemes
should be depicted distinctly in the State Accounts instead of clubbing those
under the Minor head’800’.

Chapter 1

Finances of the State Government

Profile of Rajasthan

Rajasthan is a desert State, comprising about 11 per cent of India’s
geographical land mass. During the period (2002-12), its population has grown.
by 17.20 per cent against 13.90 per cent in General Category States and the
per capita income growth (14.01 per cent) in Rajasthan has gone up as
compared to General Category States (13.09 per cent) (Appendix 1.1). In the
last ten years, the density of population in the State has increased from 165
person per square km to 201 persons. However, Rajasthan still has lower
density of population as compared to All India.

1.1 Introduction

This chapter provides a broad perspective of the finances of the Government
of Rajasthan (GoR) during 2011-12 and analyses the changes observed in the
major fiscal aggregates in relation to the previous year, keeping in view the
overall trends during the last five years. The structure and form of
Government accounts have been explained in Appendix 1.2 Part A and the
layout of the Finance Accounts is depicted in Appendix 1.2 Part B. In May
2005, Rajasthan Government responded to the Twelfth Finance Commission
(TFC) recommendation by legislating its “Fiscal Responsibility and Budgetary
Management (FRBM) Act” and as per the recommendations of Thirteenth
Finance Commission (XIII-FC) the State Government amended the Act in
March 2011 and September 2011. As prescribed in the Act, Government has
laid in legislature (March 2011) the Medium Term Fiscal Policy Statement
(MTFPS) for 2011-12. In compliance of Section 8 of FRBM Act, the State
Government constituted (February 2006) a Public Expenditure Review
Committee (PERC) under chairmanship of the Chief Secretary, GoR.

A summary of the FRBM Act, 2005 and FRBM (Amendment) Act, 2011 of
the State is given in Appendix 1.3.

1.2.1 Growth and composition of Gross State Domestic Product

Gross State Domestic Product (GSDP) is being used as a base in presenting
the trends in key fiscal parameters as well as in estimating various buoyancy
ratios especially for revenue and expenditure aggregates. The qualitative
assessment of the trends and pattern of various fiscal variables would depend
on the quality and correctness of the GSDP estimates.

1 Excluding net transactions under ways and means advances and overdraft.

Audit Report (State Finances) 2
for the year ended 31 March 2012 Lond


Finances of the State Government

Assuming that GSDP is a good indicator of the performance of the State's
economy, major fiscal aggregates like tax and non-tax revenue, revenue and
capital expenditure, internal and external debt and revenue and fiscal deficits
have been presented as percentage to the GSDP at current market prices. The
buoyancy coefficients for tax revenue, non-tax revenues, revenue expenditure
etc, with reference to the base represented by GSDP have also been worked
out to assess as to whether the mobilization of resources, pattern of
expenditure etc, are keeping pace with the change in the base or these fiscal
aggregates have also been affected by factors other than GSDP.

The trends in growth and composition of GSDP for last five years are
presented in Table-1.2.

The GSDP have shown a progressive increase over the period

sector, industries sector and services sector. The annual growth rate of GSDP
and agriculture sector in 2011-12 sharply decreased over the previous year.

1 Budget estimates and actuals

As per the State Budget Manual (1995), the Finance Department is
responsible for preparation of the annual budget for different departments.
The departmental estimates of receipts and expenditure are prepared by
Controlling Officers on the advice of the Heads of Departments and submitted
to the Finance Department by the prescribed dates. The Finance Department
then consolidates the estimates and prepares the Budget Estimates.

Finances of the State Government

Compared to the budget estimates for 2011-12, the actual revenue receipts and
revenue expenditure increased by nine per cent and three per cent
respectively. Resultantly, the revenue surplus increased by हैं 3,004 crore over
the budgeted revenue surplus. The capital expenditure decreased by 17 per
cent over the budget estimates. The budgeted and actual figures under revenue
receipts and expenditure are shown in Chart 1.1 and Appendix-1.7.

As may be observed from Chart 1.1 and Appendix 1.7 there was considerable
variation between budget estimates and actuals in the case of several key
parameters. During the year, revenue receipts had a positive variation
@ 4,724 crore: nine per cent) over budget estimates and almost all categories
of revenue receipts (with the exception of Taxes on Immovable Property other

than Agricultural land, Taxes on Goods and Passengers, other Taxes and
Duties on Commodities and Services, Water Supply and Sanitation, Police and
Public Works) were higher than budget estimates. Revenue expenditure
showed a positive variation of three per cent over budget estimates, mainly
because the expenditure in Taxes on Sales, Trade etc. Family Welfare,

Welfare of Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes and Other Backward Classes,
Social Security and Welfare, Nutrition, Relief on account of Natural
Calamities, Crop Husbandry and Power etc. exceeded the budget estimates. As
a result, the revenue surplus increased from the budget estimate of 353 crore
to® 3,357 crore and the fiscal deficit’ decreased from the budget estimate of
₹ 8,063 crore to % 3,626 crore. The estimated primary deficit (₹ 51 crore)

turned into primary surplus (% 4,266 crore). Asset creation was not given as
much priority as intended in the budget estimates as there was a shortfall of 17
per cent in Capital expenditure over the budget estimates mainly under Capital
Outlay on Water Supply and Sanitation, Capital Outlay on Urban
Development, Capital Outlay on other General Economic Services, Capital
outlay on Minor Irrigation and Capital Outlay on Major Irrigation.

1 Resources of the State

1.4.1 Resources of the State as per Annual Finance Accounts

Revenue and Capital are the two streams of receipts that constitute the State
Government’s resource base. Revenue receipts consist of tax revenues,
non-tax revenues, State’s share of Union taxes and duties and grants-in-aid
from the Government of India (Gol). Capital receipts comprise miscellaneous
capital receipts such as proceeds from disinvestments, recoveries of loans and
advances, debt receipts from internal sources (market loans, borrowings from
financial institutions/ commercial banks) and loans and advances from Gol as
well as accruals from Public Account. Table 1.1 presents the receipts and
disbursements of the State during the current year as recorded in its Annual
Finance Accounts while Chart 1.2 depicts the trends in various components
of the receipts of the State during 2006-12. Chart 1.3 depicts the composition
of resources of the State during the current year.

of the total receipts. The balance came from capital receipts, borrowings and
Public Account receipts. During 2006-12, the average revenue receipts were
₹ 38,028 crore, constituting 27 per cent of the average total receipts
@ 1,42,928 crore).

The total receipts of the State increased by 116 per cent from 2006-07 to
2011-12. Public Debt receipts (₹ 5,918 crore) constitute nearly 83 per cent of
the Capital receipts. Major share of the Public Debt (₹ 5,581 crore: 94 per
cent) was from Internal Debt.

1.4.2. Funds transferred to State implementing agencies outside the State
Budget

The Gol has been transferring a sizeable quantum of funds directly to the
State implementing agencies’ for implementation of various schemes/
programmes in Social and Economic sectors recognized as critical for the
development of Rajasthan. As these funds are not routed through the State
budget/State treasury system, the annual Finance Accounts do not capture the
flow of these funds and to that extent, State’s receipts and expenditure as well
as other fiscal variables/ parameters derived from them are underestimated. To
present a holistic picture on availability of aggregate resources, the
implementing agencies/departments were requested to furnish the details of
funds received by them directly from Gol. The information obtained is
presented in Appendix 1.8.

It was observed that the funds transferred directly to State implementing
agencies (Central Share) decreased during the current year (रे 6,838 crore)
compared to the previous year ( 8,671 crore). The overall decrease of
₹ 1,833 crore (21 per cent) was the combined effect of decrease under
Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme
(MGNREGS) (₹ 1,169 crore), Crop Compensatory Scheme (@ 679 crore),
Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (¥ 218 crore), Renovation of Co-
operative Credit Structure (९ 77 crore), Desert Development Programme (₹ 72
crore) and Up-gradation of State Government Medical Colleges (₹ 52 crore) set
off by increase under Crop Insurance 159 crore), Rashtriya Madhyamik
Shiksha Abhiyan & 94 crore), Sakshar Bharat Mission % 74 crore), Members of
Parliament Local Area Development Scheme (& 68 crore) and National Rural
Health Mission (र 63 crore).

The Gol directly transferred funds of & 6,838 crore (Appendix 1.8) to State
implementing agencies during 2011-12, out of which & 2,831 crore (41 per
cent) transferred to the Zila Parishad (Rural Development Cell) and in which
₹ 1,620 crore was only for MGNREGS. Further, Gol transferred = 1,486 crore

Direct transfers from the Gol to the State implementing agencies run the
risk of poor oversight. Unless uniform accounting practices are followed by
all these agencies and there is proper documentation and timely reporting of
expenditure, it will be difficult to monitor the proper end use of these direct
transfers.

Buoyancy ratio indicates the elasticity or degree of responsiveness of a fiscal
variable with respect to a given change in the base variable. As GSDP grows,
the ability of the Government to mobilize own tax revenue should also
increase. In 2008-10, the growth in Own Tax Resources as compared to
growth in GSDP was lower than one but from 2010-11 the situation improved
and revenue buoyancy was higher than one.

The increase of 24 per cent in revenue receipts during 2011-12 was on account
of increase in State’s own taxes (22 per cent), non-tax revenue (46 per cent),
Central tax transfers (16 per cent) and Grant-in-aid (24 per cent). The revenue
receipts were nine per cent higher than the assessment made by the State
Government in the MTFPS (₹ 52,287 crore) for the year 2011-12.

Central tax transfers

The Central tax transfers increased by € 2,121 crore over the previous year and
constituted 26 per cent of revenue receipts. The increase was under
Corporation Tax (% 870 crore), Service Tax (= 506 crore), Customs (= 349
crore), Taxes on Income other than Corporation Tax (% 339 crore), Union
Excise Duties (र 45 crore), and Taxes on Wealth (2 12 crore).

Grants-in-aid from the Government of India

The Grants-in-aid from Gol during 2011-12 increased by 24 per cent over the
previous year. The increase was under Non-Plan Grants (¥ 1,185 crore) and
Grants for Central, Centrally Sponsored Plan Schemes and Special Plan
Schemes ( 349 crore) which was counterbalanced by decrease in Grants for

1.5.2 State’s Own Resources

As the State's share in Central taxes and grants-in-aid are determined on the
basis of recommendation of the Finance Commission, collection of Central
tax receipts and Central assistance for Plan Schemes etc. the State's
performance in mobilisation of additional resources should be assessed in
terms of its own resources comprising revenue from its own tax and non-tax
resources.

1.6

Analysis of the allocation of expenditure at the State Government level
assumes significance since major expenditure responsibilities are entrusted
with them. Within the framework of fiscal responsibility legislations, there are
budgetary constraints in raising public expenditure financed by deficit or
borrowings. It is therefore important to ensure that the ongoing fiscal
correction and consolidation process at the State level is not done at the cost
of expenditure, especially expenditure directed towards development and
social sectors.

1.6.1 Growth and composition of expenditure

Chart 1.8 presents the trends in total expenditure over a period of five years
(2007-12) and its composition both in terms of ‘economic classification’ and
“expenditure by activities’ is depicted in Charts 1.9 and 1.10 respectively.

The movement of relative share of these components of total expenditure

previous year.

Revenue expenditure

The revenue expenditure constituted 87 per cent of the total expenditure
during 2011-12, while the Non-Plan Revenue Expenditure (NPRE) constituted
71 per cent of revenue expenditure and 67 per cent of total expenditure. The
revenue expenditure exceeded the assessments made by the State Government
in the MTFPS (® 51,934 crore) for the year 2011-12 by three per cent.

Similarly, NPRE increased by 14.2 per cent during the year over previous
year relative to the projections of 9.5 per cent made by the State Government
in MTFPS for 2011-12.

The Plan Revenue Expenditure (PRE) and NPRE have shown consistent
increase over the period 2007-12. The PRE increased by 41.9 per cent over
the previous year. The PRE was more than the projected increase of 16.2 per
cent in MTFPS for 2011-12.

1.6.4 Major issues relating to Local Bodies

The position of major issues related to Local Bodies i.e. Panchayati Raj
Institutions (PRIs) and Urban Local Bodies (ULBs) are summarised in
following paras:

1.6.4.1 Panchayati Raj Institutions (PRIs)
@ Introduction

There are 33 Zila Parishads (Rural Development Cell), 33 Zila Parishads
(Panchayat Cell), 249 Panchayats Samities (PS) and 9,177 Gram Panchayats
(GP) in the State as on 31 March 2012. At State level the PRIs are headed by
Pr. Secretary, Rural Development and Panchayati Raj Department (RDPRD).

(ii) Audit

The Director, Local Fund Audit Department (DLFAD) is the Primary Auditor
and the C&AG of India conducts audit under Section 14 of the CAG’s (Duties,
Powers and Conditions of Service) Act 1971. Section 75(4) of Rajasthan
Panchayati Raj Act, (RPRA), 1994 provides for test audit by the CAG of

India. Further, the State Government has entrusted (February 2011) Technical
Guidance and Supervision (TGS) over audit of PRIs by the DLFAD.

(iii) | Accounting

Till 31 March 2012, the Accounts of Local Bodies were maintained in
conventional formats prescribed under Rajasthan Panchayati Raj Rules, 1996
framed under RPRA, 1994. This was despite (i) acceptance of CAG’s formats
by RDPRD in 2003 and their further simplification in 2009 which too have
been accepted by State Government and (ii) giving of CAG’s eight data base
formats on finance of PRIs to the State Government. Despite provision in the
Rajasthan Local Fund Audit Rules, 1955, the Accounts are not being certified
by the DLFAD.

23 Audit Report (State Finances)
Lod for the year ended 31 March 2012


Finances of the State Government

(iv) Devolution

Article 243G of Constitution of India, inserted through the Constitutional
Amendment Act, (CAA) 1992 envisaged devolution of funds, functions and
functionaries to PRIs in respect of 29 subjects mentioned in XI Schedule of
the Constitution. Major orders on devolution were issued by State Government
in June 2003 and October 2010. As per information provided (May 2012) by
the PRD devolution of funds, functions and functionaries was done in respect
of 20, 28 and 20 subjects respectively (as on May 2012). However, five
functions (Drinking Water, Fuel and Fodder, Roads and Bridges, Public
Distribution System and maintenance of Community Assets) were transferred
back to their respective Departments.

(vy) Reporting

As per Section 75(4) of RDPRA, 1994, as amended through RPR
(Amendment) Act, 2011, the CAG of India may conduct test audit of accounts
and the Audit Report shall be laid before the State Legislature.

1.6.4.2 Urban Local Bodies (ULBs)
@ Introduction

There are five Municipal Corporations, 13 Municipal Councils and 166
Municipal Boards in the State headed by Principal Secretary, Local Self
Government Department (LSGD) at State Level.

(ii) Audit

The Director, Local Fund Audit Department (DLFAD) is the Primary Auditor
and the CAG of India conducts audit under Section 14 of the DPC Act 1971.
Section 99 (A) of the Rajasthan Municipal Act (RMA), 2009, as amended in
2011 provides for audit of account of municipalities by the CAG of India.

Further, the State Government has entrusted (February 2011) TGS over audit
of ULBs by the DLFAD.

(iii) Accounting

Section 90 of the RMA, 2009 contain provisions regarding maintenance of
accounts in municipalities. The Department prepared the State Municipal
Accounting Manual on the lines of National Municipal Accounting Manual in
2005. The ULBs are still in the process of completely switching over double
entry accounting system despite the orders issued by the LSGD in December
2009. The work was initially outsourced to Oswal TSF under ADB assistance
for accrual based accounting system in six ULBs and to Rajasthan Urban
Infrastructure Finance Department Corporation (RUIFDC) for 178 ULBs.
Besides, complete adoption of the eight data base formats is also yet to be
done though the same have been circulated to all the ULBs in the State.
Despite provisions in the Rajasthan Local Fund Audit Rules, 1955 the
accounts are not being certified by the DLFAD.

Audit Report (State Finances) 24
for the year ended 31 March 2012 Lod


Finances of the State Government

(iv) Devolution

Article 243G inserted through 74 CAA, 1992 envisages devolution of funds,
functions and functionaries to ULBs in respect of 18 subjects mentioned in XII
schedule of the Constitution.

Prior enactment of Rajasthan Municipal Act (RMA) 2009, the State
Government amended (1994) the RMA, 1959 inserting Section 101 (WW)(i)
and (ii) which provided for preparation of plan for Economic Development
and Social Justice by ULBs and also performance of functions and
implementation of schemes that may be entrusted by the State Government to
ULBs including those in relation to matters listed in the Twelfth Schedule of
the Constitution of India. While no specific orders were issued by the State
Government regarding devolution the Section 45 of RMA, 2009 listed core
municipal functions and provision in Section 47 of RMA, 2009 envisaged that
the State Government may by general or special order, require a Municipality
to perform such other municipal functions as the State Government may
having regard to necessity and the resources of the Municipality, think fit to be
performed by the Municipality.

(9 Reporting

As per Section 99A of Rajasthan Municipal Act, 2009 the State Government
shall cause the Audit Report under the Rajasthan Local Fund Audit Act, 1954
alongwith Technical Inspection Report of the CAG of India on Technical
Guidance and Supervision as referred in sub section (2) to be laid before the
State Legislature.

Audit Report (Civil-Local Bodies) for the year 2008-10 has been tabled in
October 2012.

Availability of better social and physical infrastructure in the State generally
reflects the quality of its expenditure. Improvement in the quality of
expenditure basically involves three aspects, viz., adequacy of the expenditure
(ie. adequate provisions for providing public services); efficiency of
expenditure use and the effectiveness (assessment of outlay-outcome
relationships for select services).

1.7.1 Adequacy of public expenditure

The expenditure responsibilities relating to the social sector and the economic
infrastructure assigned to the State Governments are largely State subjects.
Enhancing human development levels requires the States to step up their
expenditure on key social services like education and health etc. Low fiscal
priority (ratio of expenditure under a category to aggregate expenditure) is
attached to a particular sector, if it is below the respective National average.
Table 1.11 analyses the fiscal priority of the State Government with regard to

Table 1.11 indicates the following:

and social sector expenditure. Social sector expenditure (as a proportion of

priority to health and education sectors has been observed in the current
year.

3. Capital expenditure however, seems to have been given less priority in the
current year not only when compared to other General Category States but
also when compared to the priority given in 2008-09.

1.7.2 Efficiency of Expenditure Use

In view of the importance of public expenditure on development heads from
the point of view of social and economic development, it is important for the
State Governments to take appropriate expenditure rationalisation measures
and lay emphasis on provision of core public and merit goods”, Apart from

20 Core public goods are which all citizens enjoy in common in the sense that each individual's
consumption of such goods leads to no subtractions from any other individual's consumption
of that goods, e.g. enforcement of law and order, security and protection of our rights;
pollution free air and other environmental goods and road infrastructure etc. Merit goods are
commodities that the public sector provides free or at subsidized rates because an individual
or society should have them on the basis of some concept of need, rather than ability and
willingness to pay the government and therefore wishes to encourage their consumption.
Examples of such goods include the provision of free or subsidized food for the poor to
support nutrition, delivery of health services to improve quality of life and reduce morbidity,
providing basic education to all, drinking water and sanitation etc.

for the year ended 31 March 2012 Lod



Finances of the State Government

improving the allocation towards development expenditure”", the efficiency of
expenditure use is also reflected by the ratio of capital expenditure to total
expenditure (and/or GSDP) and proportion of revenue expenditure being spent
on operation and maintenance of the existing social and economic services.
The higher the ratio of these components to total expenditure (and/or GSDP),
the better would be the quality of expenditure. Development expenditure
comprised revenue and capital expenditure including loans and advances on
socio-economic services Table 1.12 and Chart 1.15 present the trends in

Share of Capital expenditure should ideally show an increase in total
expenditure since this indicates assets creation. The ratio of capital
expenditure to the total expenditure for social and economic services
increased from 15.34 in 2010-11 to 16.63 in 2011-12, indicating more
improvement in the efficiency of expenditure. Due to higher priority given to
capital expenditure, there was increase in the share of capital expenditure as a
ratio of total expenditure under Health and Family Welfare in the social
service sector and under Agriculture and Allied Activities, Power and
Transport in the economic service sector. However, less improvement was
visible in the Water Supply, Sanitation, Housing and Urban Development in
the social service sector and Irrigation Flood Control in the economic service
sector.

Ideally, the share of salary and wages should be controlled, which the State
has managed to do. The share of salary and wage in revenue expenditure of
social and economic services decreased by 4.54 percentage point from 38.95
per cent in 2010-11 to 34.41 per cent in 2011-12. The ratio of salary and wage
to revenue expenditure under social services decreased by 5.78 percentage
point from 53.12 per cent in 2010-11 to 47.34 per cent in 2011-12 mainly on
account of decrease in the share of salaries and wage under General
Education, Health and Family Welfare and Water Supply, Sanitation, Housing
and Urban Development by 2.28, 11.88 and 4.19 percentage point respectively
over the previous year.

The ratio of salary and wage to revenue expenditure under economic services
decreased by 1.99 percentage point from 14.15 per cent in 2010-11 to 12.16
per cent in 2011-12 mainly on account of decrease in the share of salaries and

Audit Report (State Finances) 28
for the year ended 31 March 2012 Lond


Finances of the State Government

wage under Transport by 3.04 percentage point over the previous year,
however, it increased in Agriculture and Allied Activities by 4.62 percentage
point.

It is important to ensure that adequate funds are allocated to operations and
maintenance since assets have to be maintained. The share of operations and
maintenance in revenue expenditure on social services and economic services
decreased by 0.18 percentage point over the previous year (1.03 per cent).
During 2011-12, only 0.85 per cent of total revenue expenditure was allotted
to operations and maintenance which was very low to preserve the assets
created.

The share of operations and maintenance decreased in revenue expenditure on
social services and economic services from 0.30 per cent and 2.30 per cent in
2010-11 to 0.24 per cent and 1.91 per cent respectively in 2011-12. The
decrease was mainly under Water Supply, Sanitation, Housing and Urban
Development, Transport and Agriculture and Allied Activities while the
increase was under Irrigation and Flood Control. During 2011-12, as per
Finance Accounts the State Government booked only = 359 crore under
Detailed Head-21-Maintenance and Repairs to maintain its public assets. The
total corpus of assets, being maintained with 359 crore, was rather large and
the limited allocation of funds may not suffice to keep assets in a good state of
repair.

Tn the post-FRBM framework, the State is expected to keep its fiscal deficit at
low levels and also meet its capital expenditure/investment (including loans
and advances) requirements. In addition, in a transition to complete
dependence on market based resources, the State Government needs to initiate
measures to earn adequate return on its investments and recover its cost of
borrowed funds rather than bearing the same on its budget in the form of
implicit subsidy and take requisite steps to infuse transparency in financial
operations. This section presents an analysis of investments and other capital
expenditure undertaken by the Government during the current year vis-a-vis
previous years.

1.8.1 Financial results of completed Irrigation Projects

The financial results of six major and 11 medium irrigation projects
(Appendix 1.9) completed with a capital outlay of = 5,074 crore at the end of
March 2012 revealed that revenue realised (₹ 61 crore) from these projects
during 2011-12 was only 1.2 per cent of the capital expenditure which was not
sufficient to cover the direct working expenses. After meeting the working
and maintenance expenditure ( 152 crore) and interest charges (रै 488 crore),
the projects suffered a net loss of 579 crore.

Indira Gandhi Nahar Project (IGNP) is the largest irrigation project under
execution in Rajasthan and various stages of it have been completed over the
years. At the end of March 2012, the capital expenditure on IGNP was हैं 3,988
crore. During 2011-12 the revenue realised from IGNP was & 22 crore
comprising just only 0.55 per cent of the capital expenditure. This revenue

Finances of the State Government

was negligible (4.80 per cent) even with reference to total working and

1.9.4 Off-Budget Borrowings

The borrowings of a State are governed under Article 293 of the Constitution
of India. In addition to the fiscal liabilities shown in Appendix 1.6, the State
guaranteed loans availed of by Government companies/corporations. These
companies/ corporations borrow funds from the market/financial institutions
for implementation of various State plan programmes projected outside the
State budget. Although the State Government projects that funds for these
programmes would be met out of the resources mobilised by these
companies/corporations outside the State budget, in reality the borrowings of
many of these concerns are repaid by the Government and ultimately turn out
to be the liabilities of the State Government termed as ‘off-budget
borrowings’. Through off-budget borro wings are not permissible under
Article 293 (3), the State continues to undertake suck off-budget borrowings.
Details of borrowings collected from Zila Parishads disclosed that during
2011-12, the Zila Parishads owed % 945.38 crore to Housing Urban
Development Corporation (HUDCO) towards its borrowings for executing
Mukhya Mantri BPL Awas Yojana. The St ate Government had undertaken to
repay the principal and interest and thus these off-budget borrowings, though
add to the fiscal liability of the State but not captured in the Finance Accounts
of the State.

1.9.6 Large number of adverse balances under DDR Heads

Adverse balances are negative balances appearing under those heads of
accounts, where there should not be a negative balance. For example, against
the accounting head of any loan or advance, a negative balance will indicate
more repayments than the original amount advanced.

As of 31 March 2012, there were 54 cases in 7 Major Heads”* amounting to
& 321.73 crore of adverse balances under Debt, Deposit and Remittances
(DDR) heads. The adverse balances were mainly under Pension Funds of
employees of Municipalities/Municipal councils (® 199.52 crore) under
‘Insurance and Pension Fund', Disbursement of Rajasthan Rajya Vidyut
Utpadan Nigam Limited (% 101.27 crore) and Disbursement of Rajasthan
Rajya Vidyut Prasaran Nigam Limited (₹ 12.16 crore) under 'Deposits of
Local Fund', Remittance to Treasury (% 3.86 crore) and Adjustable heads by
Division (₹ 2.03 crore) under 'Cash Remittance and adjustments between
officers rendering accounts to the same Accounts Officers' and Loans to
Government Servants (€ 1.72 crore).

Debt sustainability is defined as the ability of the State to maintain a constant
debt-GSDP ratio over a period of time and also indicates the ability to service
its debt. Sustainability of debt therefore also refers to sufficiency of liquid
assets to meet current or committed obligations and the capacity to keep
balance between costs of additional borrowings with returns from such
borrowings. It means that rise in fiscal deficit should match with the increase
in capacity to service the debt. Table 1.19 analyses the debt sustainability of
the State according to these indicators for the period of three years beginning
2009-10.

Table 1.19: Debt Sustainability: Indicators and Trends

Finances of the State Government

Debt Stabilisation

A necessary condition for stability states that if the rate of growth of economy
exceeds the interest rate or cost of public borrowings, the debt-GSDP ratio is
likely to be stable provided primary balances are either zero or positive or are
moderately negative. Given the rate spread (GSDP growth rate — interest rate)
and quantum spread (Debt*rate spread), debt sustainability condition states
that if quantum spread together with primary deficit is zero, debt-GSDP ratio
would be constant or debt would stabilize eventually. On the other hand, if
primary deficit together with quantum spread turns out to be negative, debt-
GSDP ratio would be rising and in case it is positive, debt-GSDP ratio would
eventually be falling.

The sum of Quantum Spread and Primary deficit was positive during 2009-12
resulting in declining trend in debt-GSDP ratio. These trends indicate that the
State is moving towards debt stabilization, which, if continued would
eventually improve the debt sustainability position of the State.

Sufficiency of Non-Debt Receipts

For debt stability and its sustainability the incremental non-debt receipts of the
State should be adequate to cover the incremental interest liabilities and
incremental primary expenditure. Debt sustainability could be significantly
facilitated if the incremental non-debt receipts could meet the incremental
interest burden and the incremental primary expenditure.

The persistent negative non-debt receipts (Resource Gap) indicate the non-
sustainability of debt while the positive resource gap strengthens the capacity
of the State to sustain the debt. The resource gap which was negative during
2009-10, turned positive from 2010-11. This meant that the State did not
depend on borrowed funds for meeting current revenue and capital
expenditure. During 2011-12, the non-debt receipts increased by & 11,996
crore (26 per cent) while the total expenditure increased by = 11,496 crore (23
per cent).

Net availability of borrowed funds

Net availability of borrowed funds is defined as the ratio of the debt
redemption (Principal + Interest Payments) to total debt receipts and indicates
the extent to which the debt receipts are used in debt redemption.

The net fund available from borrowing after providing for the interest and
repayment decreased from ~ 383 crore in 2010-11 to (-) = 617 crore in
2011-12. During the current year the available borrowed fund increased by
₹ 6,119 crore while the Government repayment increased by ~ 7,119 crore
over the previous year. Total receipts and total repayments (including interest)
of liabilities during 2011-12 were % 1,27,732 crore and & 1,28,349 crore
respectively, hence excess payment was of % 617 crore.

Interest burden

The ratio of interest payments to revenue determines the debt sustainability of
the State. During the year, interest payments reduced by two percentage points
over the previous year and constituted 14 per cent of Revenue Receipts of the
State.

investors.

Finances of the State Government

1.11

Three key fiscal parameters - revenue, fiscal and primary deficits - indicate
the extent of overall fiscal imbalances in the Finances of the State
Government during a specified period. The deficit in the Government
accounts represents the gap between its receipts and expenditure. The nature
of deficit is an indicator of the prudence of fiscal management of the
Government. Further, the way in which the deficit is financed and the
resources are applied are important pointers to its fiscal health. This section
presents trends, nature, magnitude and the manner of financing these deficits
and also the assessment of actual levels of revenue and fiscal deficits vis-a-vis
targets set under FRBM Act/Rules for the financial year 2011-12.

Fiscal deficit is the total borrowing requirement of the State and is the excess
of revenue expenditure and capital expenditure including loans and advances
over revenue and non-debt receipts. Decomposition of fiscal deficit reveals
the extent of various borrowings resorted to by the State to meet its
requirement of funds over and above revenue and non-debt receipts.

Finances of the State Government

During 2011-12, the revenue surplus (% 1,055 crore) of 2010-11 was increased
by % 3,357 crore there-by affecting the extent of fiscal deficit which could be
financed by revenue surplus. The fiscal deficit of 3,626 crore in 2011-12
was met out from net borrowing of = 7,323 crore resulting in increase in the
cash balance by हैं 3,697 crore.

It can be seen from Table 1.21 that the fiscal deficit in 2006-07 to 2011-12
was due to large net capital expenditure. Market borrowings by the State
Government continued to finance a major portion of fiscal deficit but its share
in financing fiscal deficit decreased from 117 per cent in 2010-11 to 86 per
cent in 2011-12. During 2011-12, the fiscal deficit of = 3,626 crore was
mainly met out from market borrowings (= 3,103 crore) and Small Savings
and Provident Fund etc. (€ 2,767 crore) which was counterbalanced by
increase in Cash Balance (% 3,697 crore). The market borrowings and the
small saving, provident fund etc, increased by nine per cent and 13 per cent
respectively over the previous year; thus increasing the interest burden in
future.

1.11.3 Cost of Borrowings

During the year 2011-12, the State Government raised market loans of
₹ 4,500 crore under Internal Debt. The cost of raising of this internal debt
being = 11.43 crore was 0.25 per cent of market loan taken by the State
Government. The Cost of Borrowings increased by 0.11 percentage point over
the previous year (0.14 per cent). This increased the Fiscal deficit to the extent
of € 11.43 crore.

1.11.4 Quality of Deficit/Surplus

The ratio of RD to FD and the composition of primary deficit into primary
revenue deficit and capital expenditure (including loans and advances) would
indicate the quality of deficit in the State’s finances. The ratio of revenue
deficit to fiscal deficit indicates the extent to which borrowed funds were used
for current consumption. Further, persistently high ratio of revenue deficit to
fiscal deficit also indicates that the asset base of the State was continuously
shrinking and a part of borrowings (fiscal liabilities) did not have adequate
asset backup. The bifurcation of the primary deficit (Table-1.22) indicated the
extent to which the deficit has been on account of enhancement in capital
expenditure which may be desirable to improve the productive capacity of the
State’s economy.

rising demands of the people for adequate and qualitative infrastructure. The
PPP cell established on 26 July 2007 in the Planning Department of the State
Government is the nodal agency to coordinate all efforts of the State
Government regarding development of infrastructure sectors involving PPP.

involving cost of = 13,992.66 crore (Appendix 1.13) relating to Road, Power,

Finances of the State Government

110 Recommendati

The State Government has shown a return to fiscal consolidation by
showing a healthy growth in both tax and non-tax revenue. As a result the
revenue and primary surplus increased by € 2,302 crore and = 1,023 crore
respectively over the previous year and fiscal deficit was a healthy one per
cent of GSDP as against the target of three per cent as prescribed by revised
FRBM Act for 2011-12. Some concerns regarding the State's finances are
as follows:

Revenue Receipts: During 2011-12, the State’s revenue receipts (¥ 57,011
crore) increased by 24.1 per cent over the previous year. The growth in
revenue receipts was mainly due to improvement in both tax revenue and non-
tax revenue. The State’s revenue receipts as a percentage of GSDP marginally
declined from 15.8 per cent in 2007-08 to 15.5 per cent in 2011-12, which
indicates that the growth of tax revenue and non-tax revenue had not kept
pace with that of GSDP. Government should mobilise additional resources
through tax and non-tax revenue by expanding the tax base and rationalising
the user charges.

Revenue expenditure: Of the total expenditure of हैं 61,882 crore during
2011-12, the revenue expenditure (RE) constituted 87 per cent of the total
expenditure while the Non-Plan Revenue Expenditure constituted 77 per cent
of RE. Nearly 58 per cent of the revenue receipts of the State was spent on
committed expenditure such as salaries and wages, interest, pension and
subsidies leaving little for other purposes. There is a necessity to contain the
committed expenditure.

Budget estimates

The actual revenue receipts and revenue expenditure increased by nine
per cent and three per cent respectively as compared to the budget estimates
for 2011-12. Resultantly, the revenue surplus increased by हैं 3,004 crore over
the budgeted revenue surplus. The capital expenditure decreased by 17 per
cent over the budget estimates. The estimated primary deficit turned into
primary surplus. The actual fiscal deficit decreased from the budget estimate
by हैं 4,437 crore.

Inadequate Priority to Capital expenditure: The capital expenditure
increased by 36 per cent over the previous year and constituted 11.5 per cent
of total expenditure against previous year 10.4 per cent. It was 17 per cent
lower than that projected in MTFPS for 2011-12. However, the ratio of capital
expenditure (11.5 per cent) as a proportion of aggregate expenditure in the
current year was lower than 2007-08 (18.2 per cent). The progressive capital
expenditure (₹ 68,553) at the end of March 2012 includes € 7,993 crore (11.7
per cent) blocked on 160 incomplete projects. Government should give
adequate priority to capital expenditure since the capital expenditure was
lower than the projections of State Government in its MTFPS. The State
Government should formulate guidelines for quick completion of incomplete
projects to avoid time and cost over runs with a view to take corrective action.
Increase in asset creation even if it involves an increase in fiscal deficit
(within the bounds of three per cent) may be to the advantage of the state.

45 Audit Report (State Finances)
lod for the year ended 31 March 2012


Finances of the State Government

Financial Management

Review of Government investments: The average return on State
Government’s investment in Statutory Corporations, Rural Banks, Joint Stock
Companies and Co-operatives varied between 0.2 to 0.4 per cent in the past
three years while the Government paid an average interest of 7.7 per cent on
this investment. As of 31 March 2012, investment of € 7,806 crore (56 per
cent) was made in Statutory corporations, rural banks, Government companies
and joint stock companies having accumulated loss of € 4,641 crore.

It would be advisable for the State Government to ensure better value for
money in investments, otherwise high cost borrowed funds will continue to be
invested in projects with low financial return. Projects which are justified on
account of low financial but high socio-economic return may be identified and
prioritized with full justification as to why high cost borrowings should be
channeled there. It would also be prudent to review the working of state public
sector undertakings which are incurring huge losses and work out either a
revival strategy (for those which can be made viable) or closed down (if they
are not likely to be viable given the current market conditions).

Prudent cash management: As on 31 March 2012, the State had a surplus
cash balance of € 9,785 crore. It increased by 61 per cent over the previous
year. The cost of holding surplus cash balances is high. Since, maintaining
huge idle cash balance is not prudent cash management, appropriate steps
should be taken for spending on capital projects for creation of assets.

Transfer of funds to State implementing agencies

Oversight of funds transferred directly from the Gol to the State
implementing agencies: During 2011-12, the Gol directly transferred हैं 6,838
crore to the State Implementing Agencies compared to हैं 8,671 crore during
the previous year. It was seen that a major portion i.e हैं 2,831 crore (41 per
cent) went to Zila Parishads (Rural Development Cell) and ₹ 668 crore (10
per cent) to Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan. Direct transfer of funds from the Gol to
the State Implementing Agencies runs the risk of improper utilisation of funds
by these agencies.

As long as these funds remain outside the State budget, there is no single
agency monitoring its use and there is no readily available data on how much
is actually spent in any particular year on major flagship schemes and other
important schemes which are being implemented by State implementing
agencies but are funded directly by the Gol. A system has to be put in place to
ensure proper accounting of these funds and the updated information should
be validated by the State Government as well as the Principal Accountant
General.

A review of release and utilization of grants recommended by XIII-FC by
some Departments revealed non-utilisation of grant of रैं 265 crore against
sanctioned amount of € 404 crore by eight departments due to non-finalisation
of action plan/work plans.

for the year ended 31 March 2012 Lod


Chapter 2

Financial Management and Budgetary Control

Appropriation Accounts are accounts of the expenditure, voted and charged, of
the Government for each financial year compared with the amounts of the
voted grants and appropriations charged for different purposes as specified in
the schedules appended to the Appropriation Acts. These Accounts list the
original budget estimates, supplementary grants, surrenders and re-
appropriations distinctly and indicate actual capital and revenue expenditure
on various specified services vis-d-vis those authorized by the Appropriation
Act in respect of both charged and voted items of budget. Appropriation
Accounts thus facilitate management of finances in monitoring budgetary
provisions and are, therefore, complementary to Finance Accounts.

Audit of appropriations by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India seeks
to ascertain whether expenditure actually incurred under various grants is
within the authorization given under the Appropriation Act and that the
expenditure required to be charged under the provisions of the Constitution is
so charged. It also ascertains whether the expenditure so incurred is in
conformity with the law, relevant rules, regulations and instructions.

As per the State Budget Manual (SBM), the Finance Department is
responsible for the preparation of the annual budget by obtaining from the
various departments material on which to base its estimates. The departmental
estimates of receipts and expenditure are prepared by Controlling Officers on
the advice of the Heads of Departments and submitted to the Finance
Department by the prescribed dates. The Finance Department then
consolidates the estimates and prepares the Detailed Estimates and Grants.
The estimates of expenditure should be as accurate as possible. An avoidable
excess in an estimate is as much a financial irregularity as an excess in the
actual expenditure. The budget procedure envisages that the sum provided in
an estimate of expenditure on a particular item must be that sum which can be
expended in the year and neither larger nor smaller. A saving in an estimate
constitutes as much of a financial irregularity as an excess in it.

The SBM was last published in 1995. After fifteen years, in July 2010, the
State Government entrusted the work of revision of SBM to a Private Limited
Company for completion by 1 November 2011. However, the revised SBM
approved by the cabinet was stated to be under printing (September 2012).
Further, while the copies were awaited, the SBM was stated to have been
uploaded on the website’ on 27 September 2012.

Deficiencies in management of budget and expenditure and violation of
Budget Manual noticed in audit have been discussed in the subsequent
paragraphs.

Reasons for savings occurred under the above Grants are discussed below:

0 Grant number 3-“Secretariat”

The savings (© 356.84 crore) occurred mainly under head "3451-Secretariat-
Economic Services-Other expenditure-Through the Planning Department-
State level New Schemes" in which the entire provision of € 300 crore was
surrendered/re-appropriated at the end of the year due to non-designing of
structure and forming of new projects.

o Grant number 8-“Revenue”

The savings (® 137.31 crore) under "2029-Land Revenue-Land Records-
District expenditure (© 50.98 crore) were mainly due to posts remaining
vacant in various cadres. This was against the provisions contained in Para
53(7)(i) and (ii) of SBM. Besides, savings in Modernisation of Land
Settlement Department (€ 68.63 crore) were mainly due to absence of policy
decisions and necessary sanctions at State level resulting in non-starting of
survey work under National Land Record Modernisation Programme
(NLRMP).

o Grant number 9-“Forest”

The savings (© 134.70 crore) under "2406-Forestry and Wild Life-
Environmental Forestry and Wild Life-Wild Life Preservation-Tiger Project,
Ranthambhore (¥ 84.58 crore) and Tiger Project, Sariska (¥ 16.81 crore)" was
attributed mainly to non-receipt of funds from Government of India (Gol) for
relocation of the families in sanctuary areas.

न Grant number 15-"'Pesions and other Retirement Benefits"

Savings (© 224.24 crore) under the grant occurred mainly in "2071-Pensions
and other Retirement Benefits-Superannuation and Retirement Allowances-
Pensions to State employees" due to non-submission of final expenditure
report in time by the Treasury Offices.

. Grant number 24-“Education, Art and Culture”

The savings (© 514.12 crore) occurred under "2202-General Education-
Elementary Education-Assistance to Block Panchayats/ Intermediate level
Panchayats" (© 319.03 crore) mainly due to posts remaining vacant. This was
against the provisions contained in Para 53(7)(i) and (ii) of SBM. Besides,
savings (© 293.87 crore) under "Secondary Education-Government Secondary

In Grant No. 03, the persistent savings ranged between ₹ 19.09 crore and
% 356.84 crore of the total budget during 2009-12. The main reasons of savings
were posts remaining vacant and non-payments of arrears of salary as per
recommendations of Sixth Pay Commission during 2009-10 and posts
remaining vacant and non-receipt of appropriate proposals by State Planning
Board for recommendation of the schemes during 2010-12.

In Grant No. 09, the persistent savings ranged between = 66.03 crore and
₹ 134.70 crore of the total budget during 2009-12 in Revenue Section and was
mainly due to posts remaining vacant and non-receipt of funds from
Government of India. Besides, the persistent savings ranging between & 20.23
crore and ₹ 59.30 crore in Capital Section during 2009-12 was due to
reduction in plan ceiling under different projects for Forest/CAMPA and
Development of Keviadev National Park.

In Grant No. 19, the persistent savings ranged between ₹ 22.33 crore and
₹ 109.81 crore of the total budget during 2009-12. The main reasons for saving
were slow progress of works under various construction projects of Public
Works Department.

In Grant No. 23, the persistent savings ranged between ₹ 1.28 crore and ₹ 3.83
crore of the total budget during 2009-12. The main reasons for savings was
reduction in plan ceiling under Training and less receipt of funds from the Gol
under High level Industrial Training Institute during 2009-10 and non-receipt
of sufficient tenders, less receipt of material from the firms, some of the
materials supplied by the firm were rejected due to technical reasons and non-
fulfillment of terms and conditions of World Bank by the Suppliers under
Vocational Training Improve ment Project during 2010-12.

In Grant No. 27, the persistent savings ranged between % 593.18 crore and
= 1,071.44 crore of the total budget during 2009-12. The main reasons for
saving were slow progress of works under various water supply schemes.
Reasons for final savings in 2011-12 were not intimated by the Government
(August 2012)

In Grant No. 29, the persistent savings ranged between = 186.83 crore and
= 354.54 crore of the total budget during 2009-12. The main reasons for saving
were reduction in plan ceiling and less receipt of funds from Government of
India in 2009-10 and 2010-11 and due to non-release of grants to Rajasthan
Urban Development Fund, less receipt of funds from the Government of India
and delay in payment of bills of Bisalpur Project in 2011-12.

In Grant No. 33, the persistent savings ranged between ₹ 7.95 crore and
% 21.83 crore of the total budget during 2009-12. The savings were mainly due
to reduction in plan ceiling, less receipt of funds from Gol and less execution
of works in 2009-10. Besides, savings in 2010-12 were mainly due to non-
availability of land, reduction in plan ceiling, non-starting of works and less
execution of construction works by Public Works Department.

In Grant No. 35, the persistent savings ranged between = 17.22 crore and
% 708.14 crore of the total budget during 2009-12. The savings were mainly
due to deposit of unutilised amount of “Rajasthan State Investment Fund”

2.3.3 Excess expenditure

In 12 cases, the programme delivery Departments exceeded the expenditure
limits set by the Legislature through grants (¥ 64.65 crore). Such excesses are
irregular and they require retrospective Legislative sanction through
appropriate vote. In these 12 cases, excess expenditure was more than = one
crore and also by more than 10 per cent of the total provisions in each case
(Appendix 2.2). However, in three cases the overall excess expenditure (₹ one
crore and above in each case) has been observed consistently for the last three
years (Table 2.9).

Table 2.9: List of Major heads indicating persistent excess expenditure during 2009-12

crore)

2.3.4 Excess over provisions relating to previous years (2008-11) requiring
regularisation

As per Article 205 of the Constitution of India, it is mandatory for State
Government to get the excess over a grant/appropriation regularised by the
State Legislature. Although no time limit for regularisation of expenditure has
been prescribed under the Article, the regularisation of excess expenditure is
done after completion of the discussion of Appropriation Accounts by the
Public Accounts Committee (PAC). However, the excess expenditure
amounting to = 499.55 crore for the years 2008-11 was yet to be regularised
(31 August 2012). The year-wise amount of excess expenditure awaiting
regularisation for grants/appropriations is summarized in Table 2.10. Grant/
Appropriation-wise details of excess expenditure requiring regularisation are
given in Appendix 2.3.

2.3.6 Unnecessary/excessive supplementary provision

Para 183 of SBM stipulates that if during the currency of a financial year, the
amount provided for any purpose is found to be inadequate or a need arises for
an expenditure on some object or service for which no provision has been
made, a supplementary provision can be sanctioned by the Legislature.
Supplementary provision aggregating to ₹ 413.14 crore obtained in 10 cases,
amounting to % one crore or more in each case, during the year proved
unnecessary as the expenditure did not come up to the level of original
provision as detailed in Appendix 2.4. Important cases where supplementary
provisions proved unnecessary are summarised below:

In Grant No. 34-Relief from Natural Calamities, provision of = 300.41 crore
was obtained in March 2012 through second supplementary for transfer of
outstanding second installment of = 300.33 crore on account of State Disaster
Response Fund for the year 2010-11 was unnecessary as the actual expenditure
was even less than the original provision.

In Grant No. 19-Public Works, supplementary provisions of = 3.52 crore
obtained for construction of Jails under the recommendations of XIII Finance
Commission proved unnecessary as the entire provision was surrendered on 31
March 2012; reasons for which have not been intimated (August 2012) by the
Government. Besides, supplementary provisions of = 8.85 crore obtained by
the Department for construction of building of State Excise proved
unnecessary in view of anticipated savings of ₹ 15.38 crore due to less
execution of works. Supplementary provision of = 10.90 crore obtained for
construction of Judicial building under the recommendations of XIII Finance
Commission proved excessive in view of total saving of = 9.38 crore due to late
receipt of sanction for construction work.

Thus, supplementary provisions were made without assessing the actual
requirements under the sub-heads.

2.3.7 Excessive/unnecessary re-appropriation of funds

Re-appropriation is transfer of funds within a grant from one unit of
appropriation, where savings are anticipated, to another unit where additional
funds are needed. Injudicious re-appropriation proved excessive, unnecessary
or insufficient resulting in excess of = 94.84 crore in 104 sub-heads and
savings of = 446.57 crore in 313 sub-heads. The re-appropriation and final
excess/saving was more than = one crore in 52 sub-heads as detailed in
Appendix 2.5. Out of these cases, there was insufficient re-appropriation in 21
heads of account, unnecessary re-appropriation in 17 heads of account and
excessive re-appropriation in 14 heads of account. It indicates that the
estimates were not properly assessed, as even after the
withdrawal/augmentation of funds through re-appropriation there were final
savings/excesses in the grants

2.3.8 Unexplained re-appropriations

According to Para 194 of SBM, reasons for the additional expenditure and the
savings should be explained in the re-appropriation statement and vague
expressions such as ‘based on actual requirements’, ‘based on trend of
expenditure’, etc., should be avoided. However, a scrutiny of re-appropriation
orders issued by the Finance Department revealed that out of 2151 sub-heads,
the explanations have been received in respect of 821 sub-heads for additional
provision/withdrawal of provision, in which 72 sub-heads explanations were
of general nature like ‘actual requirement’, ‘based on latest assessment’ and
‘restriction of expenditure’. Besides, in 1330 sub-heads no specific
explanation was given. This also goes against the principle of transparency
stipulated in Section 4 of Fiscal Responsibility and Budget Management
Act.

2.3.9 Anticipated savings not surrendered

As per para 138 of SBM, the spending departments are required to surrender
the grants/appropriations or portion thereof to the Finance Department as and
when the savings are anticipated.

During 2011-12, out of total saving of हैं 6,537.39 crore an amount of
= 6,078.92 crore (93 per cent) was surrendered on the last working day of
financial year indicating lack of realistic financial planning and weak financial
control which resulted in deprivation of funds for other development purposes.

Out of total savings of % 4,357.92 crore under 20 grants (savings of € one
crore and above) amount aggregating to ₹ 512.64 crore (11.8 per cent of total
savings) were not surrendered, details are given in Appendix 2.6.

Besides, in 39 cases, (surrender of funds in excess of = 20 crore in each case),
& 5,911.32 crore were surrendered on the last working day of March 2012
(Appendix 2.7). Departments did not furnish (August 2012) any

63 Audit Report (State Finances)
Lond for the year ended 31 March 2012


Financial Management and Budgetary Control

reasons/explanations regarding surrender of savings in excess on the last
working day of financial year.

2.3.10 Lump-sum provision

As stipulated in Para 53(6) of SBM, the spending departments are required to
furnish full details of expenditure which should be shown in the estimates and
no lump-sum provision should be made in the estimates. During 2011-12, in
79 cases (77 grants and two appropriations) a lump-sum provision of
1,742.31 crore was made in the estimates in violation of this stipulation.
However, % 1,573.72 crore (90.3 per cent) of this provision remained
unutilised at the close of the financial year, which were surrendered at the fag
end of the year, details are given in Appendix 2.8 and balance amount was
re-appropriated. Government had not stated (August 2012) any reasons for
making lump-sum budget provisions under these cases.

2.3.11 Surrender in excess of actual savings

In seven grants and one appropriation, the amount surrendered (₹ one crore or
more in each grant) was in excess of actual savings indicating lack of
inadequate budgetary control in these departments. As against savings of
% 434.74 crore, the amount surrendered was % 492.11 crore resulting in excess
surrender of = 57.37 crore (Appendix 2.9). Departments did not furnish any
reasons/explanations regarding surrender in excess of actual savings (August
2012).

2.3.12 Rush of expenditure

According to Para 139 of SBM, rush of expenditure in the closing month of
the financial year should be avoided. During 2011-12, 17 per cent of total
expenditure was incurred in the month of March. However, in respect of 61
sub-heads listed in Appendix 2.10, expenditure (exceeding & ten crore and also
more than 50 per cent of the total expenditure in each case) of & 3,557.20
crore was incurred in last quarter of the financial year, which was 76 per cent
of total expenditure. Of this हैं 3,403.73 crore (73 per cent) was spent in March
2012. Thus, a substantial amount incurred by the department at the fag end of
the year is indication of poor financial control over the expenditure.

Outcome of Inspec

There are 265 treasuries/sub-treasuries in the State, which are under purview
of inspection by Office of the Principal Accountant General (A&E),
Rajasthan, Jaipur. Major irregularities noticed by the Office of the Principal
Accountant General (A&E), Rajasthan while conducting inspection of
treasuries/sub-treasuries during the period 2011-12 are as follows:

* Non-disposal of confiscated items of gold and silver

As per note 2 below Rule 122 of Rajasthan Treasury Rules, 1999, Jaipur
(City) Treasury has been declared by GoR as Reserve Treasury for depositing
gold, silver, diamond and coins etc. which has become the property of State
Government after their confiscation.

It was observed that detailed instructions for disposal and accounting of such
items has not been laid down by the Director, Treasury and Accounts,
Rajasthan despite approval given by the State Finance Department.
Consequently, confiscated gold and silver jewellery of 23.642659 Kilogram
and 758.43338 Kilogram respectively were lying un-disposed and idle since
last 23 years.

* Incomplete maintenance of Pension Cheque Register on computers

Director, Treasury and Accounts, Rajasthan instructed (April 1998) all the
Treasury Officers to maintain a pension check register for checking pension
payments made through Public Sector Banks for ascertaining excess/short
pension payments. It was observed that 31 treasuries/sub-treasuries did not
maintain the required pension check register with complete details.

* Incomplete maintenance of Broadsheets of Long Term Loans

As per the instructions issued (May 1988) by the State Finance Department,
all columns of Broadsheets for long term loans and advances viz. House
Building Advances and Conveyance advances should be filed in properly.

However, it was observed that 14 treasuries did not maintain the broad sheets
of long term loans and advances with complete details as number and date of
sanctions issued, number and amount of monthly instalments, treasury
voucher number and date were not noted therein, balance not brought forward.
and broadsheets were not closed at the month end.

* Keeping of valuables in the Security Rooms having no guards

As per the instructions (July 2003) issued by the State Finance Department
and Rule 6 of Rajasthan Treasury Rules 1999, wherever there were no
arrangements of guard in Sub-treasuries, the District Collectors will not issue
sanctions for keeping the Sealed Packets/valuables therein. During inspection
it was noticed that in 14 sub-treasuries, various authorities kept sealed packets
in security rooms having no guards.

for the year ended 31 March 2012 Lod


Financial Management and Budgetary Control

+ Non-disposal of outdated/damaged stamps

Rule 319 of Rajasthan Treasury Rules 1999, provides sending a report of
outdated/damaged stamps to Inspector General Stamps and Registration Ajmer
and disposals of outdated stamps should be done under his permission. During
inspection it was noticed that 10 treasuries/sub-treasuries did not take action
for disposal of such outdated/damaged stamps of % 20.12 lakh.

* Non-obtaining of security bond paper from Cashiers

Rule 313 of General Financial and Accounting Rules envisages that
Collectors/Treasury Officers should ensure that adequate security bond is
obtained from working cashier. It was noticed that in 28 treasuries/sub-
treasuries the work regarding cash was being performed by the cashier without
obtaining security bond from him.

2.7 Expenditure on New Services without approval of the

Legislature

The expression “New Service” occurs in Article 115(1)(a) and 205(1)(a) of
Constitution of India. Item No. 21 of Para 16 of SBM defines New Service as
a new policy decision (not brought to the Legislature’s notice earlier)
including a new activity or form of investment. Further, large expenditure
exceeding five lakh or 20 per cent of the existing budget provision under
sub-head or, where there is no sub-head under a Minor head, whichever is
more, arising out of extension of an existing activity shall also be treated as
New Service. Expenditure on ‘New Service’ shall be met from the
Contingency Fund during the course of the financial year.

In 10 grants (37 cases) an expenditure of = 88.80 crore was incurred during
2011-12 on New Services i. e. the expenditure was more than 20 per cent of
the budget provision and exceeded & five lakh in each case (Appendix 2.11).
In all these cases expenditure on New Services was not met from the
Contingency Fund and was incurred without the approval of Legislature.

1101 and Recom:

During 2011-12, there was an overall saving of & 6,537.27 crore against the
total grants and appropriations of f 73, 253.50 crore. The overall savings were
the net result of saving of % 6,537.39 crore offset by excess of F 0.12 crore,
which requires regularisation under Article 205 of the Constitution of India.
Large savings indicated deficient budget estimates. The departments
surrendered % 6,079 crore (93 per cent) and the entire surrender was made on
the last working day of the financial year leaving no scope for utilising these
funds for other development purposes. In seven cases, हैं. 57.37 crore was
surrendered in excess of savings. In 79 cases a lump-sum provision of
₹ 1,742.31 crore was made in the estimates in violation of provision of SBM.
Of this, = 1,573.72 crore (90 per cent) remained unutilised.

Financial Management and Budgetary Control

Rush of expenditure at the end of the year is another chronic feature noticed in
the overall financial management, which indicates lack of budgetary control.
In 37 cases the expenditure of € 88.80 crore was incurred on New Services

Review of some grants also revealed that (i) there were persistent savings
ranging between € 0.10 crore and % 94 crore in Forest, Medical and Health,

(ii) though the Tourism Department had been making provision every year in

Recommendations:

* Efforts should be made by all the departments to submit realistic budget
estimates keeping in view the trends of expenditure and actual requirement
of both plan and non-plan funds, in order to avoid large scale
savings/excess, re-appropriations and surrenders at the end of the year.

* Savings are to be worked out before hand and surrendered before the close
of the financial year for its effective utilisation in other areas/schemes.

+ The department should follow more reliable mechanism in budgeting to
avoid persistent savings or excess.

+ The Government may closely monitor the expenditure against the
allocations in all departments and avoid excess expenditure over the
grants.

+ Expenditure against allocations should be spaced out and closely
monitored in order to achieve the desired goals; else budgeting will
remain an exercise on paper.

* Expenditure on New Service should be met from Contingency Fund and
bear approval of the Legislature.

+ Functioning of Treasuries should be monitored to avoid their deficient
functioning.

Chapter 3

Financial Reporting

This Chapter provides an overview and status of the State Government’s
compliance with various financial rules, procedures and directives during the
current year.

specific purposes, Utilisation Certificates (UCs) should be obtained by the
departmental officers from the grantees and after verification, these should be
forwarded to the Principal Accountant General (A&E) within 12 months from
the date of their sanction unless specified otherwise. However, 15,557 UCs
due in respect of grants and loans aggregating to € 2,925.71 crore paid during
1997-98 to 2010-11, 331 UCs (2.13 per cent) for an aggregate amount of
& 15 crore were in arrears. The department-wise break-up of outstanding UCs
